@@137680171 <p> At a virtual public comment hearing hosted by the US Environmental Protection Agency on Thursday , a long line of environmental advocates voiced strong opposition to proposed new regulations weakening requirements that utilities must follow in cleaning up toxic coal ash residue at hundreds of sites across the country at which coal was burned to produce electricity . <p> " The Trump administration has jeopardized the nation 's drinking water supplies as a favor to polluters , " Lisa Evans , senior counsel at Earthjustice and a former EPA attorney , said in a statement . " It 's just not right . " <p> The Trump administration announced in April that it would repeal a rule put in place in 2024 by the Biden administration 's EPA that required utilities to monitor coal ash sites at inactive coal plants . The Trump EPA also said it would loosen requirements for protecting groundwater near those sites . Now the Trump administration wants to rely on states for coal ash monitoring and enforcement and enable them to bypass national standards in some cases . <p> In announcing the new proposed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " commonsense changes " and said they " reflect EPA 's commitment to restoring American energy dominance , strengthening cooperative federalism , and accommodating unique circumstances at certain coal ash facilities . " <p> The proposed rule would exempt sites where coal ash is stored from regulation and permit coal-fired power plant owners to minimize , delay , or avoid dealing with the coal ash at their facilities . <p> Coal ash , or coal combustion residuals , is the mineral residue left after burning coal to generate electricity . It contains potentially toxic levels of substances like mercury , arsenic , and lead , all of which are associated with human health problems , including cancer . <p> More than half of the fine , gray , powdery residue is used each year to create concrete , drywall , or other industry applications . This is often called " beneficial use " by the coal industry . <p> A 2022 study by Earthjustice and other environmental groups found that more than 90 percent of coal power plants across America were contaminating groundwater via coal ash residues . <p> At @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a spokesman for the American Coal Ash Association lauded the Trump rollbacks as the right move forward . <p> John Ward , whose trade group focuses on advancing the management of materials made from coal ash , said the association is in support of the EPA 's move to eliminate criteria defining " beneficial use " for coal ash . He called coal ash an underutilized domestic mineral resource . Coal ash can be useful in the production of cement , wallboard , agriculture , and potentially critical minerals , he said . <p> Coal ash can improve concrete strength and durability , while supporting the supply chain for critical construction materials , said Leah Pilconis , vice president of government affairs and general counsel at the American Cement Association . <p> That trade group also supports the EPA 's proposed provision changes . Among them : redefining coal ash for cement manufacturing not as an industrial waste but instead as a part of the cement production process . The proposed changes come as supply of coal ash declines , Pilconis said , and could improve access to legacy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ used to make cement and other materials , vast quantities of the toxic residue are kept on-site at both active and retired coal plants , where it 's often covered with water or soil to prevent it from contaminating the air or waterways . The EPA has long had concerns about these sites : In 2002 , the agency reported that improper lining on these coal ash ponds and landfills allow toxins to leach into the groundwater . <p> In their comments on Thursday , environmental groups said the EPA 's proposed rule guts protections against the dangers of burning coal and puts the nation 's groundwater at risk . Existing rules were built upon years of science , litigation , and documented harm , they said . <p> Jennifer Cassel , another attorney with Earthjustice , said water near coal ash becomes thick with pollution , like a tea that is steeped for too long . <p> Cassel has been working on protecting communities from coal ash pollution for 15 years and said rain and hurricanes amplified by climate change have exacerbated these threats . And those who live @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ discover cancer at a rate that makes them think , " This can not be normal . " <p> " EPA , you know the record , " Cassel said . " You made the record . " <p> Kristina Zierold , a professor at the University of Mississippi , said she has found that children exposed to coal ash are more likely to suffer from depression and have poorer school performance than children who are n't exposed . <p> Zierold said she has been researching the health impacts of coal ash on children since 2011 and was awarded a National Institutes of Health grant in 2015 to investigate coal ash and neurobiological health in children 6 to 14 years old . <p> She and her research team utilized air pollution and dust sampling in the homes of children to collect coal ash and tested children for neurobehavioral and mental health conditions in multiple ways . <p> If a child performs poorly in school , that can have cascading effects through adulthood , Zierold said . Depression in children can lead to poor social interaction , lack of learning , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Do you want your children playing on coal ash in parks and playgrounds ? " Zierold asked . " Do you want them breathing it in and ingesting it ? I do n't . " <p> Brianna Knisley , the director of public power campaigns at Appalachian Voices , said the 2008 Kingston Fossil Plant coal ash spill was one of the worst industrial disasters in US history . It 's an example of what happens when the EPA leaves coal ash management up to state regulators and utilities , she said . <p> The 900 workers who cleaned up the spill were denied protective gear and told the coal ash they were working to remove was clean enough to eat . Hundreds of workers became sick and dozens are dead , Knisley said . <p> Angie Mummaw , an organizer with Appalachian Voices who lives near the Cumberland Fossil Plant in Tennessee , said she 's tired of communities like hers being treated as sacrifice zones while the coal industry asks for permanent loopholes instead of cleaning up the messes they 've created . <p> Knisley has worked @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's ball fields and seen Tennessee Valley Authority waste piles of the toxic ash piled up behind a public playground , open to the wind . @@137686866 <p> A fungus is always thought to be a harmful organism when they exist in humans and animals , as well as plants . But fungi -- organisms that include yeasts and molds that get their nutrition by absorbing dissolved organic molecules , secreting digestive enzymes into their environment -- take a bad rap . <p> In agriculture , they play a dual role , acting both as destructive crop pathogens and as essential partners for soil health and plant growth . Fungal infections can destroy between 20% and 40% of global annual harvests , costing billions of dollars and posing massive threats to food security . <p> Managing them effectively requires balancing the suppression of harmful fungal diseases against the cultivation of beneficial fungi to maximize yield and sustainability . Those that are helpful to agriculture are microscopic powerhouses used to improve crop yields , boost nutrients naturally , and reduce dependence on synthetic chemicals . <p> Some fungi naturally target and kill agricultural pests without relying on harsh chemicals , and help to break down organic matter , turning crops . <p> Researchers at the Hebrew University have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can produce higher yields and better taste in specific crops . This method improves the firmness and natural sugar content of tomatoes , which today are often tasteless , as well as melons and corn , while significantly boosting production . <p> A collage portraying the potential of plant beneficial microorganisms , exemplifying the fungus Pseudozyma aphidis and their secreted compounds as an eco-friendly alternative to the harmful synthetic plant growth-enhancing products utilized in agriculture . ( credit : Anton Fennec ) <p> Their discovery offers a practical way to meet global food demands without compromising the health of the planet or the quality of produce . Because the approach uses stable microbial secretions instead of live cultures , it ensures consistent and reliable performance across various agricultural environments and climates . <p> The natural , eco-friendly way to significantly increase agricultural yields while also improving the quality and taste of produce was developed by Prof. Maggie Levy and researchers Anton Fennec and Neta Rotem . <p> They have just published their research in the journal Plant Physiology under the title " Make it grow : Pseudozyma aphidis extract promotes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Agriculture Ministry . <p> As the global population continues to grow , the demand for higher agricultural output has historically led to the heavy use of synthetic fertilizers and pesticides . These chemicals often contribute to soil and water pollution and increase greenhouse gas emissions . The new research suggests that beneficial microorganisms can offer a sustainable alternative to these traditional agricultural inputs . <p> While previous studies have shown that live cultures of certain fungi can help plants grow , the application of live organisms in large-scale farming is often difficult due to varying climate conditions and the unpredictability of how the colonies will establish themselves on different hosts . <p> To solve this , the team focused on using the extract rather than the live fungus itself . This method eliminates the dependency on living fungal colonies and leads to more consistent , repeatable results in the field environment . The results showed improvements across nearly every stage of the plants ' development . <p> Levy told The Jerusalem Post that treated tomato seeds displayed an 18% increase in germination rate , while corn and melon seeds increased @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to two weeks earlier than those in the control group . The treated tomato plants produced over 60% more ripe fruit by weight compared to the control group . Melon plants showed a weight increase five times greater than the untreated plants . <p> They saw that beyond the mass and quantity , the produce of the treated plants was of higher quality than the control . Tomatoes were found to be firmer and received higher scores in sensory taste tests for sweetness and aroma . <p> The researchers found that the fungus promotes growth through several mechanisms , including the production of auxin-like molecules that are natural plant hormones , as well as siderophores , which help plants absorb iron from the environment . It kills pathogens , increases immunity , and promotes growth . <h> Valuable tool for green agriculture <p> The study highlights the potential for this extract to serve as a valuable tool for green agriculture . By improving the efficiency of plant growth and the nutritional value of the produce , this approach supports the broader goal of global food security without the environmental footprint @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the extract of compounds secreted by Pseudozyma aphidis acts as an effective agent for enhancing both the quantity and the quality of agricultural produce , " said Levy . " By using a natural extract rather than live cultures , we can provide farmers with a more reliable and eco-friendly tool to increase yields and improve the flavor of the food on our tables . " <p> She added that " feeding the increasing global population requires higher agricultural yields while avoiding the use of harmful products . Beneficial microorganisms provide an eco-friendly alternative to synthetic fertilizers and pesticides . <p> " But the rapid growth of the global population and the constant demand to increase the agricultural yield require an intense use of fertilizers and pesticides . Fertilizers are a major source of nitrous oxide , which is a greenhouse gas contributing to global climate change . " <p> Prof. Maggie Levy ( credit : HEBREW UNIVERSITY ) <p> The team will continue refining the extraction process to identify the specific compounds responsible for these growth-promoting effects . <p> Levy became interested in farming because she was raised in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , who grew pecans , avocados , and mangos and raised chickens . <p> This is not Levy 's first innovation . She has been involved in three companies already . One is PsyRx , which is developing an innovative treatment for depression . The patented drug it developed combines an FDA-approved selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor used to treat depression , anxiety , and other mood disorders with microdoses of ibogaine . <p> The company uses proprietary bioreactor and tissue-culture technology to cultivate and extract highly consistent psilocybin ( from Psilocybe fungi ) and extract ibogaine from the tissue of the Tabernanthe iboga Africana tree . The drug aims to shorten the time to therapeutic effect , reduce side effects , and improve the quality of life for patients . <p> The second company she co-founded eight years ago , based on research in mammary cell biology , is BioMilk ( now Wilk ) , the first publicly traded company aiming to produce cultured cow and human breast milk from cells in a bioreactor . It focuses on culturing mammalian cells to produce animal-free dairy components , including proteins , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fungal extract genuinely different from previous biological solutions , Levy explained that " it 's different because we do n't use live organisms , but since it 's an extract , it is more consistent and stable and is n't affected by the environment as much as live organisms and can be controlled better . <p> " If this technology succeeds commercially , it will improve not only crop yields and tastes , but it will also pollute the land and water less and be more economical . " Many imported tomatoes today are picked when they 're premature and sometimes still green , so they are less tasty when they reach supermarkets , " she noted . The fungus can be sprayed on leaves , added to the soil , or used on seeds , which could be sold with the material already included . <p> This can minimize the amount needed to do the job . The extract , she stated , remains stable in extreme heat or drought conditions , so it would n't be affected by climate change . <p> She is optimistic that the extract @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be a boon to farmers . <p> Levy said she had no idea how much it would cost . " It would depend on the company that commercializes it , but it certainly will be cheaper than the chemicals used today . "

A slide from the city 's Land Use Bureau spells out the energy initiative , which will be reviewed by the city Zoning Board . <p> courtesy of Stamford Land Use Bureau <p> STAMFORD -- A new initiative to make buildings in Stamford " greener " and cleaner could be coming to the city 's booming skyline , putting Stamford on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ solar panels and " green roof " plantings on large new construction projects . <p> A " scorecard " for energy efficiency on new building projects , which would require developers working on large new structures to earn a passing grade by installing energy-efficient systems , is also being planned by the city 's land-use agencies . <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> At a recent meeting of the city 's Planning Board , Land Use Bureau Chief Ralph Blessing said the city 's planning and zoning staff were putting into action the goals of the comprehensive plan adopted last year by city leaders that made energy efficiency a priority . The initiative would change the text of the zoning code and impact future development projects . <p> " We have worked on a text change to make Stamford more sustainable , it 's a very important goal of the city 's comprehensive plan . We think we will get us a little bit closer to the sustainability goals the comprehensive plan has laid out , " Blessing told the Planning Board . <p> The energy and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continues below this ad <p> Stamford Advocate Logo <h> Want more Stamford Advocate ? <p> Make us a Preferred Source on Google to see more of us when you search . <p> All new structures with roofs exceeding an area of 5,000 square feet would be required to install solar panels or a " green roof " lined with plants and vegetation . The panels or vegetation would have to cover half the roof area . In cases where " amenity spaces " are in place , a smaller amount of solar panels or vegetation would be allowed . <p> According to the narrative from the Land Use Bureau on the proposed zoning amendment , " Solar roofs greatly contribute to the production of emission-free energy , thus helping to address climate change . Green roofs can help with stormwater management , sequester carbon dioxide , provide habitat and reduce the urban heat island effect . The purpose of this section is to increase the amount of solar and green roofs throughout the City of Stamford . " <p> In addition to those requirements , new buildings with ten or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more of gross floor area , would be required to submit a " scorecard " that gives points on nine different categories of energy efficiency and related systems . New construction projects would have to score at least a ' B ' grade to pass , while renovation and reconstruction projects would require a ' C. ' <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> Blessing said the scorecard system has been a requirement for developers for the past few years , mainly to encourage clean energy as an aspirational goal , but it would come with a mandatory pass-fail process in the future should the zoning amendment be adopted . The scorecard would be based on a point system examining nine different building functions , with energy efficiency given the highest number of points . <p> " We put a high focus on energy use , that 's the main goal we 're interested in , making buildings more energy efficient , " he said . <p> Planning Board member Jeremy Linder asked if the clean energy initiative might discourage developers from Stamford and interfere with the construction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to make the process as smooth as possible and not to impede good development projects from going forward . " We tried to keep it simple , " he said , " And buildings with high energy efficiency , they pay for themselves in the long run . " <p> Advertisement <p> Article continues below this ad <p> The city 's planning director said there had been a range of opinions from developers on the scorecard policy , which has been in effect in a preliminary capacity for several years . Some developers were enthusiastic about it and complained when their projects were n't given an ' A ' rating , Blessing said , while others treated it as " a nuisance . " <p> The proposed zoning amendments would also adopt a more stringent policy for preservation of trees at development sites -- the third part of the environmental initiative . The regulations would require developers to re-plant trees lost to construction , with the same amount of circumference equal to or greater than that of the trees that were removed . I-STORM now works @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ engineers across the globe can request a peer review of their flood control infrastructure , as New Orleans has done . <p> The advice shared can range from softer skills -- how to effectively communicate flood risk -- to the literal nuts and bolts of maintaining a flood control system . ( Staff photo by Brett Duke , The Times-Picayune ) <p> STAFF PHOTO BY BRETT DUKE <p> The Dutch have valuable expertise to impart . They now spend $1.4 billion annually through the Delta Program , its major flood control initiative . <p> And they 've been in the flood control game for almost a millennium . Since the Middle Ages , in their own Rhine-Meuse-Scheldt river delta , the Dutch have shaped their landscape to control water @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ attraction than flood control mechanism , that are the Renaissance-era wind-powered forerunners of the kind of pumping stations New Orleans now relies on . <p> The Netherlands and New Orleans have much in common when it comes to flood protection . Both are low-lying , and parts of each would be submerged below water if not for major feats of engineering . But the Dutch created their flood control system first . <p> Hurricane Katrina hit New Orleans in 2005 , but the Dutch 's defining storm hit in 1953 , which killed more than 1,800 people . Before that storm , the country was already aware that its flood infrastructure could not protect the country from a massive storm . In the 1930s , studies indicated that the Dutch would be swamped in a storm of the size that ultimately hit the country , but World War II delayed upgrades to its flood control system . <p> When the Dutch did so , they created one of the first modern flood control systems , one that New Orleans would emulate after Katrina hit . New Orleans ' Hurricane and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of pumps , levees and surge barriers that helps to limit the impact of storm surge on the region -- is largely inspired by the system the Dutch built . <p> But recently , the flow of information has turned upriver , in a sense , from New Orleans back to the Netherlands . <p> " Katrina made us aware that we ca n't deliver full protection , " Walraven said . " When Katrina happened here , the Dutch government and our organization realized that we went too far in promising safety against flooding . " <p> Thursday , October 27 , 2005 Aerials : A ship passes the gates of the Maeslant storm surge barrier as it enters the New Waterway from the North Sea . The gates swing closed during storms to protect over a million people in and around Rotterdam during storm surges . <p> Michael DeMocker The Times-Picayune <h> ' Up to politics ' <p> The framing of New Orleans ' flood control system as a " risk reduction system " -- one that reduces risk rather than provides full protection -- was a crucial @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " I think the Dutch feel very much protected , " he said . " But we 're now more aware that we have to stress personal responsibility as well . They have to think about evacuation . Katrina was the incentive for us to become aware of that . " <p> During a news conference while Walraven was in New Orleans , Southeast Louisiana Flood Protection Authority-East Regional Director Jeff Williams stressed the importance of his agency 's partnership with the Dutch . <p> " It 's a constant , ongoing learning process , " Williams said . <p> He said when a system is being designed , it 's hard to know how it will age , what kind of maintenance will be required down the line , and what challenges may come with actually operating it . <p> " We learned maintenance from them , " said Williams . " They learn about some of the new technology that did n't exist 50 years ago from us . " <p> The Dutch have a clear-eyed relationship to climate change and the future of their coastline . Walraven @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 've built is not prepared to withstand future sea level rise , and they 're readying to build " adaptive storm surge barriers . " What those will look like is n't yet clear . <p> " That 's something we are now going to discover in the next one , two decades , " Walraven said . " The studies that are now taking place in Galveston Bay , or Copenhagen , or Singapore , or New York -- they are designing systems that will have to answer these types of questions and develop new ideas for future design . " <p> What is clear is that the upgrades will be costly . The Dutch plan to spend $31 billion or more on improvements through their Delta program through 2050 . <p> New Orleans ' flood control system is designed to withstand a 100-year storm surge , or a storm with a 1% chance of occurring each year . The region is pushing for a study on how to improve the system so that it can withstand a 200-year storm surge , seen as crucial for keeping the city @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The Dutch also recognize that adapting to a changing climate may involve giving some land back to the sea . They envision different scenarios -- they might raise their levees and build more pumps , but they might also abandon low-lying areas in favor of fortifying their cities . They have already done so in certain flood-prone areas . <p> What to protect and what to abandon ? Walraven says that " will be up to politics . @@137704878 <p> Members of the buoy deployment team leave the area after the deployment of one of four buoys in Lake Maurepas . Scientists from Southeastern Louisiana University will be monitoring the marine life populations ( fishes , crabs , shrimp ) , as well as the plant life in the surrounding wetlands , and also watching and studying any variations in water quality with data from the buoys . <p> Three years ago , the administration of then-Gov . John Bel Edwards finished a broad plan to cut the state 's greenhouse gas emissions through 2050 and help industries transition to low-carbon production . <p> That plan is still technically on the books despite the state 's right-wing shift under Gov . Jeff Landry , and lawmakers hoping to ditch it altogether are learning that doing so is not a simple matter . <p> Landry , who has questioned human-caused climate change , has staked out a quiet middle ground -- not supporting the plan 's emissions reductions but remaining open to related projects that his administration says have " broad support . " <p> This position has run headlong @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a method of mitigating climate change by injecting carbon dioxide emissions permanently underground . <p> LSU scientists say it is safe technology , and big Louisiana industries see it as a way to lower the carbon intensity of their fossil fuel-based operations without giving up on oil and gas . There are also major federal tax credits in place to support it . <p> But critics worry about threats to groundwater , pipeline leaks and land rights infringements from the deep injection wells and their infrastructure . They are asking how Louisiana has gotten to the point where nearly three dozen carbon capture projects are up for review . <p> Enter state Rep. Charles " Chuck " Owen , R-Rosepine , a retired military intelligence officer , climate change skeptic and CCS critic . Owen has called for the repudiation of the 2022 Climate Action Plan . <p> Owen says the plan never received the Legislature 's scrutiny , commits to a Paris climate agreement that the second Trump administration dumped in January 2025 and remains a justification cited in permits for CCS projects he opposes . <p> Earlier @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ resolution calling for an oversight hearing . <p> Owen said he wants to expose the " shenanigans " that led to the adoption of climate goals that , he says , give businesses a false impression of what Louisianans want . <p> " I 'm trying to recalibrate where we are in this state , " he said . <p> Owen said he wants to shift to a more full-throated endorsement of oil and gas development , though he is n't opposed to renewable projects that can stand on their own economically . The House Natural Resources and Environment Committee would hold the hearing , possibly this fall , the committee chairman has said . <p> The panel includes members skeptical of climate change and oppose CCS , but also has a separate majority which has blocked most bills to bar or limit CCS . The technology is drawing billions of dollars in new industrial investment to Louisiana , and these members see it as critical for oil , gas and petrochemical industries , including the fast-growing LNG industry , to make low-carbon products sought in Europe and Asia . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of defending some of the plan 's proposed solutions while not endorsing the science behind climate change itself . <p> State Rep. Jessica Domangue , R-Houma , who sits on the committee , said there 's no question in her mind that the climate has been changing , but she is n't sure it is tied to manmade emissions versus natural cycles . <p> Domangue , 43 , said she grew up in a family that lived off the shrimping industry and struggled through the oil bust of the 1980s . Her votes to protect carbon capture are aimed at supporting her oil and gas-reliant district in Terrebonne Parish , she said . <p> She said countries want low-carbon products from wherever they can find them , and worries what kind of message killing the climate plan would send . <p> " I know that companies are watching every piece of legislation that 's coming out of this body , and they 're watching , and they want to make sure that we 're continuing to move towards the goal of these low-carbon emissions , whether we like it or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ away ' <p> Since 2018 , about $100 billion in CCS-related projects have been proposed for Louisiana , state economic officials say . <p> " Whether Trump likes it or not , whether I like it or not , this is where we are . This is the world that we 're living in . It 's not going away , " Domangue added . <p> A consensus developed by years of scientific study has concluded the climate is warming from manmade emissions from burning fossil fuels . Seas have been rising for years , a change expected to sharpen in the coming decades in Louisiana , driven by warming temperatures and land subsidence . <p> Domangue 's coastal district near Houma could see between 3.3 feet and 8.5 feet sea level rise by 2100 , according to projections from the National Oceanographic and Atmospheric Administration . <p> Developed from a consensus of industry , environmental groups , scientists and others meeting publicly over 15 months , the 2022 Climate Action Plan laid out the scale of the state 's greenhouse gas emissions and committed to achieving net-zero emissions by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate agreement , " net zero " is meant to keep global temperatures from rising further than they have already during the world 's industrial era . <p> The state plan does n't have the force of law , but anticipates emissions reductions through CCS , coastal protection , increased industrial electrification , more efficient gas-fired power plants and several other methods . <h> ' Sky would not fall ' <p> Charles Sutcliffe , Louisiana 's first chief resilience officer under Edwards , said his task force to develop the plan included legislative appointees and demonstrated that a consensus could be reached from diverse interests -- and " that Louisiana could speak the words ' climate change ' out loud and the sky would not fall . " <p> Development of the plan 's science-based recommendations " involved over 140 experts and nearly 50 public meetings , " he added . <p> Members of the buoy deployment team leave the area after the deployment of one of four buoys in Lake Maurepas . Scientists from Southeastern Louisiana University will be monitoring the marine life populations ( fishes , crabs , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the surrounding wetlands , and also watching and studying any variations in water quality with data from the buoys . <p> Provided photoby Randy Bergeron <p> " It also showed that by doing that hard work , we could attract billions of dollars in federal grants to both reduce our disaster risk and improve our infrastructure , and we could send a signal to the private sector that Louisiana was ready to lead the next generation of energy production just like it had the last , " said Sutcliffe , now a senior resilience adviser with the National Wildlife Federation . <p> " And we could do it all with an eye on improving people 's health and lives . I am not sure which parts of that need repudiating . " <p> As CCS storage and CO2 pipeline projects have begun to roll out , however , the usual political dynamic around environmental issues has been scrambled , with left-leaning environmental groups finding uneasy common cause with rural conservatives generally in favor of oil and gas to oppose CCS . <p> Louisiana 's oil and gas industry and chemical @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ oil majors spent decades casting doubt on climate change . <p> The Louisiana Mid-Continent Oil and Gas Association and Louisiana Chemistry Association , which favor carbon capture , did not respond to requests for comment . The Louisiana chapter of the Sierra Club , which opposes carbon capture , opposed Owen 's resolution . <p> On Edwards ' way out in 2023 , he expressed hope that Landry would stick with the plan . Landry has n't formally acted to undo it , and his administration has regularly lauded new economic development with CCS or other low-carbon components . <p> But , in testimony earlier this month before the House environment committee , Dustin Davidson , secretary of the state Department of Conservation and Energy , disavowed the plan 's emissions reduction goals . He said the state does n't use the plan to guide " environmental policy decisions . " <p> He also did n't oppose repudiating the state plan . But he warned about climate mitigation projects in the plan , pointedly noting a biomass power plant that Owen supports for his House district . <p> " There @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ broad support over , so it 's very critical that we understand everything that 's in it before we go out and say , ' Throw all this out . Let 's not do anything that 's in here , ' " Davidson said May 6 . <p> Those comments elicited a response from Owen that he supports some of the projects in the plan , including the one in his district , but believes the overall blueprint should have had legislative review . <p> State Rep. Neil Riser , R-Columbia , who backs a CCS-tied project in his district , suggested an oversight hearing to lay out " the pros and cons . " <p> The next week , the House environment committee backed a resolution from Owen calling for that hearing . House Resolution 274 cleared the House on Wednesday without opposition .
